A framework for quantum theory of elementary physical entities
Wen-ge Wang

Abstract
A unified framework, which is directly established on the quantum ground, is proposed for elementary physical entities, called modes in this paper. The framework is mainly built upon five basic assumptions, which loosely speaking have the following contents. (i) The state space of each mode is given by the direct product of a momentum-state space and a spinor-state space, the latter of which is certain representation space of the S ​ L ​ ( 2 , C ) SL(2,C) group (a covering group of the Lorentz group); (ii) spinor states of modes have a layer-type structure and modes are either fermionic or bosonic, depending on their helicity properties; (iii) there are three fundamental processes — free evolution, vacuum fluctuation (emergence or vanishing of a pair of fermionic modes that possess exactly opposite physical properties), and two fundamental interaction processes (change of two fermionic modes into one bosonic mode and the reverse); (iv) vacuum fluctuation happens instantly; and (v) the time evolution operator is constructed from operators, which map state spaces of incoming modes of fundamental processes to those of outgoing modes. The time evolution operator turns out to be a function of quantum fields that are constructed from creation and annihilation operators for free-mode states, whose interaction part has a local feature. As an example, a simple model of modes is studied and is compared with the first-generation part of the standard model (SM). Concerning electroweak interactions, the studied model has a time evolution operator, whose main body is formally similar to that of the SM. Besides, it predicts 1 3 \frac{1}{3} and 2 3 \frac{2}{3} electronic changes for quark-type modes, gives an interpretation to the color degree of freedom, and contains certain modes that behave like dark matters.



I
Introduction

One of the ultimate goals of physics is to establish a theory, in which elementary physical entities and
their interactions may be described in a unified way.
This goal is partially achieved in the standard model (SM), the most successful quantum field theory (QFT)
that has ever been built (see textbooks, e.g., Refs.
Weinberg-book
;
Peskin
;
Itzy
),
in which the electromagnetic and weak interactions are described in a unified way
based on the local gauge group of
U(1)\otimes SU(2)
, known as the Glashow-Weinberg-Salam (GWS) electroweak theory,
meanwhile, the strong interaction is described based on the group of
SU(3)
in an independent way.
The SM still has some unsatisfactory features and the topic of going beyond the SM
has attracted lots of attention (see, e.g., Refs.
GGS99
;
KO01
;
Polch-book
;
Ross84
;
Raby93
;
book-fundam-QFT
);
for example, a satisfactory explanation to neutrino masses
is still lacking
Fukuda98
;
Ahmad02
;
Araki05
;
Adamson08
;
An12
;
Ahn12
;
Abe12
;
Abe14
;
GN03
;
SV06
.

In this paper, a framework is proposed for developing theories in which elementary physical entities
may be described in a unified way.
It is directly built on a quantum ground, different from QFTs which need to start from classical fields.
To distinguish from the terminology used in the SM,
we use the name of
“mode”
to refer to an elementary physical entity that lies at the fundamental level.

The framework is based on five basic assumptions (BAs).
One BA states that mode states consist of only two parts — a momentum-state part and a spinor-state part.
Here, spinor-state spaces are given by representation spaces of the so-called
SL(2,C)
group
(a covering group of the proper and orthochronous Lorentz group

Penrose-book
;
Kim-group
;
CM-book
;
Corson
).
This BA implies that properties of the spinor spaces used
should be sufficient for characterizing differences among mode species.
Another BA states that vacuum fluctuation (VF) is a fundamental process,
which is described in the most direct way, as emergence/vanishing of two modes
that possess exactly opposite physical properties from/into the vacuum.
Ideas behind the rest assumptions are as follows:
Modes are either fermionic or bosonic, depending on their helicity properties;
there are two fundamental interaction processes, as the change of
two fermionic modes into one bosonic mode and the reverse;
VF happens instantly;
and the time evolution operator is derived from operators that map
state spaces of incoming modes to those of outgoing modes of fundamental processes.

Upon the above-discussed five BAs and with the help of a few additional assumptions, we are to
show that many basic properties of state spaces of modes and of the time evolution operator may be determined.
In particular, the time evolution operator is found to have a concise form, written as a function of
quantum fields that are constructed from creation and annihilation operators for free-mode states.
A framework is thus established for developing theories for elementary physical entities.
An interesting question is whether this framework may allow the construction of a specific model,
which does not contain so many assumptions as the SM, meanwhile,
whose time evolution operator has a form similar to that of the SM.
We are to show that such a model is constructable.

The paper is organized as follows.
For the sake of easy reading, in Sec.
II
,
we give a short list for notations and conventions to be used.
Detailed contents of the five BAs are given in Sec.
III
,
and state spaces of modes are discussed in Secs.
IV
-
VI
.
The time evolution operator is discussed in Sec.
VII
.
The above-mentioned specific mode is given in Sec.
VIII
,
and its similarities to and differences from the first-generation part of the SM
are discussed in Sec.
IX
.
Finally, summary and discussions are given in Sec.
X
.

II
Notations and conventions

It proves convenient to use Dirac’s ket-bra notation for spinor states,
particularly in derivations related to the time evolution operator.
Such an abstract notation system for Weyl spinors has been discussed in detail in Ref.
pra16-commu

and its main points are given in Appendix
A.1
of this paper.
The abstract notation for four-component vectors is discussed in Appendix
A.3
,
after a brief discussion of their basic properties in the ordinary notation in Sec.
A.2
.
A brief discussion of
SL(2,C)
transformations are given in Appendix
B
.

In this section, we list major conventions, notations, and abbreviations to be used.
We give here brief explanations to them and leave detailed discussions (if needed) to later sections.

1. Conventions
.

(i) An
overline
above a spinor indicates its complex conjugate
and similar for symbols related to spinors.

1
1

1

This is a convention usually used in the mathematical theory of spinors.
Under this convention, e.g.,
\overline{U}
for a Dirac spinor
U
refers to
its complex conjugate, but not to
U^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}
.

(ii) Repeated index implies a summation over it,
unless otherwise stated.

(iii)
|\psi\phi\rangle\equiv|\psi\rangle|\phi\rangle
and its bra is written as
\langle\phi\psi|\equiv\langle\phi|\langle\psi|
.

2. Frequently used notations for spinors
.

(a)

{\mathscr{W}}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
: the two smallest nontrivial representation spaces of the
SL(2,C)
group,
spanned by two-component Weyl spinors, which are the complex conjugate space of each other.

(b)

|S^{A}\rangle
of
A=0,1
: a basis in the space
{\mathscr{W}}
.
Its complex conjugate, as a basis in
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
, is written as
|\overline{S}^{A^{\prime}}\rangle
with a primed index
A^{\prime}=0^{\prime},1^{\prime}
.

(c)

|\kappa\rangle=\kappa_{A}|S^{A}\rangle
: an expansion of an arbitrary Weyl spinor
|\kappa\rangle\in{\mathscr{W}}
,
with expansion coefficients
\kappa_{A}
.
The corresponding spinor
\overline{|\kappa\rangle}
in
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}

is expanded as
\overline{|\kappa\rangle}=\overline{\kappa}_{A^{\prime}}|\overline{S}^{A^{\prime}}\rangle
.

(d)

\epsilon^{AB}
and
\epsilon_{AB}
: symbols for raising and lowering indices of spinors in
{\mathscr{W}}
, respectively,
both having the matrix expression of
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right)
.
For example,
\kappa^{A}=\epsilon^{AB}\kappa_{B}
and
\kappa_{A}=\kappa^{B}\epsilon_{BA}
.
The corresponding symbols for
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
are written as
\epsilon^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}
and
\epsilon_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}
, respectively,
described by the same matrix.

(e)
{\mathscr{V}}
: a four-component vector space, which is isomorphic to
{\mathscr{W}}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
.
A basis in
{\mathscr{V}}
is written as
|T_{\mu}\rangle
with
\mu=0,1,2,3
.

(f)
|S_{AB^{\prime}}\rangle
: a shorthand writing of
|S_{A}\rangle|\overline{S}_{B^{\prime}}\rangle
.

3. Spinor bra for scalar product.

We introduce spinor bras for writing
SL(2,C)
-scalar products of spinors in an abstract notation,
and call them
spinor bra for scalar product
.
As seen in the explicit expression of the scalar product of Weyl spinors (as elementary spinors),
which is given in Eq.(
287
) of Appendix
B
,
the product involves no complex-conjugated components.

2
2

2

This is different from the case of inner product for vectors in a Hilbert space.
In fact, to introduce
c
-number products in a theory in which the
SL(2,C)
group lies at the fundamental level,

SL(2,C)
-scalar product is the one that should be introduced first, while, inner product (with Hilbert space)
may be introduced at a later stage.

For this reason, no complex conjugate component should be involved in the definition of
spinor bra for scalar product, in contrast to bra used in inner product.
To indicate this difference explicitly, we write the former as
\langle\langle\cdot|
with double “
\langle
”,
while using the ordinary notation for the latter.
Below are explicit notations for bras.

(a)

\langle\langle S^{A}|
: the spinor bra for scalar product, which corresponds to the ket spinor
|S^{A}\rangle
,
giving a basis in a space dual to
{\mathscr{W}}
.
The scalar product of
|S^{A}\rangle
and
|S^{B}\rangle
is written as

\displaystyle\langle\langle S^{A}|S^{B}\rangle=\epsilon^{AB}.

(1)

(b)

\langle\langle\kappa|
: the spinor bra for scalar product, which corresponds to the spinor
|\kappa\rangle
.
The scalar product of two Weyl spinors
|\kappa\rangle
and
|\chi\rangle
, written as
\langle\langle\kappa|\chi\rangle
,
has the expression in Eq.(
287
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\langle\langle\kappa|\chi\rangle\equiv\kappa_{A}\chi^{A}.

(2)

Equation (
2
) requires the following expansion of
\langle\langle\kappa|
,

\displaystyle\langle\langle\kappa|=\langle\langle S^{A}|\kappa_{A},

(3)

which has the same expansion coefficients as the ket
|\kappa\rangle
, not involving complex conjugation.

(c)
\langle\langle\Psi|
: the spinor bra for scalar product, which corresponds to
a generic ket spinor
|\Psi\rangle
.
If
|\Psi\rangle
is expanded as

|\Psi\rangle=c_{1}|\Psi_{1}\rangle+c_{2}|\Psi_{2}\rangle
, then, the bra
\langle\langle\Psi|
is written as

\displaystyle\langle\langle\Psi|=c_{1}\langle\langle\Psi_{1}|+c_{2}\langle\langle\Psi_{2}|,

(4)

consistent with Eq.(
3
).

(d)
\langle\widehat{\psi}|
: the bra of a ket spinor
|\psi\rangle
,
which is obtained by certain
operation that involves complex conjugation (ACC operation to be discussed below).
It may be used in the construction of inner product.

4. Notions for modes and their states.

(i)
M
: a generic mode.

(ii)
F
: a generic fermionic mode.

(iii)
B
: a generic bosonic mode.

(iv) Basic mode: a mode whose spinor space is either
{\mathscr{W}}
(called
b
-mode) or
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
(called
\overline{b}
-mode).

(v)
|M^{\alpha}_{{\bf p}}\rangle
: a state of a mode
M
with a three-momentum
{\bf p}
and an index
\alpha=(r,\varrho)
,
where
r=0,1
is an index for helicity

3
3

3

The helicity index
r
is not influenced by the operation of complex conjugation.

and
\varrho
indicates the sign of
p^{0}
of a four-momentum
p^{\mu}
.

(vi)
|\overline{\Psi}\rangle
:
a shorthand writing for the complex conjugate of
{|\Psi}\rangle
, i.e.,
|\overline{\Psi}\rangle=\overline{|\Psi\rangle}
,
except for
\Psi=M,f,B
, and
{\bf p}
.

(vii)
|w^{r}({\bf p})\rangle,|u^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
, and
|v^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
: helicity states of
b
-mode.

(viii) Layer structure: a structure of spinor states of modes, expressed as a one-column matrix
with one row corresponding to one layer (under certain restriction to be
given in BA2 of the next section).

(ix)
f
: a fermionic mode, whose first layer lies in a space
{\mathscr{W}}
and is described by
|u^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
.

(x)
|{\bf p}\rangle
and
\langle{\bf p}|
: ket and bra of momentum state, as in the ordinarily-used notation.

5. Overlines not indicating complex conjugate
.

(a)
\overline{M}
: the antimode of
M
.
Two modes are the antimode of each other, if their layers have a one-to-one correspondence
and spaces of corresponding layers have the relationship of complex conjugation.

(b)
|\overline{M}^{\alpha}_{{\bf p}}\rangle
: a state of a mode
\overline{M}
.

(c)
\overline{f}
: the antimode of
f
, with the first layer state as
|\overline{v}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
.

(d)

\displaystyle\qquad\overline{{\bf p}}:=-{\bf p}\ \ \qquad\text{for three-momentum},\qquad

(5a)

(e)

\displaystyle\qquad\overline{\alpha}:=(r,-\varrho)\ \ \qquad\text{for $\alpha=(r,\varrho)$}.\qquad

(5b)

6. Some abbreviations
.

(i) BA
i
: the
i
-th basic assumption (BA).

(ii) FIP: fundamental interaction process.

(iii) VF: vacuum fluctuation.

(iv) BPIP: basic positive-
p^{0}
interaction process.

(v) MA: assumption used in a specific model.

(vi)
d\widetilde{p}
: a symbol used for an integration over
{\bf p}
,

\displaystyle d\widetilde{p}:=\frac{1}{|{\bf p}|}d^{3}p.

(6)

(vii) ACC operation: a combined operation of taking antimode-counterpart state and complex conjugation.

III
Basic assumptions

In this section, we give five BAs, as the foundation of the framework to be proposed.
We first discuss two BAs about mode states in Sec.
III.1
,
then, in Sec.
III.2
discuss a strategy to be used for constructing dual state spaces,
and, finally, give the rest three BAs about fundamental processes in Sec.
III.3
.

III.1
Basic assumptions for states of modes

In this section, we introduce two BAs concerning basic properties of states of modes.

The first BA, indicated as BA1, is about the global structure of mode states.
According to experiences that have been accumulated by experimental and theoretical
studies of the physical world during the past several hundreds of years,
states of physical entities should possess at least two types of degree of freedom —
one related to position and the other to spin —
and obey the well known Lorentz symmetry.

At the fundamental level of theory, the simplest method of respecting the Lorentz symmetry
is to assume that state spaces of modes may be given by representation spaces of the
SL(2,C)
group,
a covering group of the proper and orthochronous Lorentz group.
Elements in these representation spaces are called
spinors
.
In fact, the theory of spinors supplies
the most powerful mathematical tool for dealing with the Lorentz symmetry
Penrose-book
;
Kim-group
;
CM-book
;
Corson
.

As is well known in quantum mechanics,
the position representation and the momentum representation
are mathematically equivalent, linked by a Fourier transformation.
But, when establishing the foundation of a theory that emphasizes spin — more exactly helicity states,
it proves convenient to use the momentum representation,
because the helicity of a particle should be computed with respect to a given three-momentum.

One basic idea of this paper is that
modes possess two types of degree of freedom only,
one related to momentum and the other related to spinor.
Since there is no essential difference among momenta of difference physical entities,
the above idea requires that spinor properties should be sufficient for distinguishing among mode species.

Summarizing the above discussions, we state BA1 as follows.

•

BA1
. The state space of a mode consists of only two parts: a momentum-state part and a spinor-state part,
the latter of which is a representation space of the
SL(2,C)
group.

We use
|{\bf p}\rangle
to indicate a momentum state with a three-momentum
{\bf p}
,
and use
{\cal E}_{\rm mom}
to denote the space spanned by all
|{\bf p}\rangle
.
The spinor space of a mode
M
is denoted by
{\cal E}^{S}_{M}
.
The state space of
M
, indicated as
{\cal E}_{M}
, is
then given by their direct product, i.e.,
{\cal E}_{M}={\cal E}_{\rm mom}\otimes{\cal E}^{S}_{M}
.
The space dual to
{\cal E}_{\rm mom}
is spanned by the ordinary bras
\langle{\bf p}|
,
for which the well-known Lorentz-invariant inner product is written as

\displaystyle\langle{\bf p}^{\prime}|{\bf p}\rangle=|{\bf p}|\delta^{3}({\bf p}-{\bf p}^{\prime}).

(7)

The second BA, indicated as BA2, is about the spinor structure of modes.
As is known in the theory of spinors, there are two smallest nontrivial representation spaces of
SL(2,C)
,
which we indicate by
{\mathscr{W}}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
.
Other representation spaces are constructed from
{\mathscr{W}}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
.
We call modes, whose spinor spaces are
{\mathscr{W}}
or
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
,
basic modes
.
More exactly, we call a basic mode with
{\mathscr{W}}
a
b
-mode
and
that with
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
an
\overline{b}
-mode
(pronunc anti
b
-mode).
Moreover, a mode is called a
unit mode
,
if its spinor space is
{\mathscr{W}}
, or
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
, or a direct product of some of
{\mathscr{W}}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
.

BA2 basically states a layer structure of spinor states,
which refers to a one-column matrix, whose rows are given by spinor states of unit modes.
We call a row in such a matrix a
layer
.
Physically, it is natural to require that all the layers within a same mode should
possess the same helicity.
Besides, like the spin-statistics relationship established in QFT
Pauli40
;
SW64
,
we relate half-integer spin to fermionic mode and integer spin to bosonic mode, satisfying

\displaystyle|F\rangle|F^{\prime}\rangle=-|F^{\prime}\rangle|F\rangle\quad

\displaystyle\text{for fermionic modes},

(8a)

\displaystyle|B\rangle|B^{\prime}\rangle=|B^{\prime}\rangle|B\rangle\quad

\displaystyle\text{for bosonic modes}.

(8b)

To summarize, we state BA2 as follows.

•

BA2
. Each mode’s spinor state has a layer structure,
with all its layers possessing a same helicity;
the mode is fermionic if its layers have a half-integer helicity, otherwise, is bosonic.

Note that, under the requirement of identical helicity of all the layers within one mode,
a layer structure does not exactly correspond to a direct sum.
(Different rows in the matrix form of a direct sum are independent.)

III.2
About a space dual to
{\cal E}_{M}

A problem is met in the construction of inner product, or a like, for vectors in
the state space
{\cal E}_{M}
of a generic mode
M
.
The problem is that no definite direct product may be formed from
a space, which is spanned by spinor bras for scalar product
[e.g., spanned by
\langle\langle S^{A}|
in Eq.(
3
)],
and the space spanned by the ordinary momentum-state bras
\langle{\bf p}|
.
This is because expansion coefficients in the former do not involve complex conjugation
[see Eq.(
4
)], while, those in the latter do.

4
4

4

As an illustration, one may consider
|\Psi\rangle=|\psi_{1}\rangle|\psi_{2}\rangle
, where

|\psi_{1}\rangle=a(c_{0}|S^{0}\rangle+c_{1}|S^{1}\rangle)
as the spinor part and
|\psi_{2}\rangle=\frac{1}{a}|{\bf p}\rangle
as the momentum part,
with
c
-number parameters
a\neq 0,c_{1},c_{2}
.
One finds that the bra in the space spanned by
\langle\langle S^{A}|
is written as

\langle\langle\psi_{1}|=a(c_{0}\langle\langle S^{0}|+c_{1}\langle\langle S^{1}|)
,
while, the momentum-state bra is
\langle\psi_{2}|=\frac{1}{a^{*}}\langle{\bf p}|
.
Thus, although
|\Psi\rangle
is independent of the number
a
,
the product of
\langle\langle\psi_{1}|
and
\langle\psi_{2}|
is proportional to
a/a^{*}
.

To solve the above-discussed problem, one method is to make use of some operation
that maps
{\cal E}^{S}_{M}
to another space of ket, which involves complex conjugation in certain way.
Then, one may map the new space to a space of spinor bras for scalar product,

5
5

5

In fact, a related problem is met in the relativistic quantum mechanics
when constructing inner product for Dirac spinors,
though usually formulated in a different mathematical language.
The solution given there makes use of a transformation like
U^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}
in the ordinary terminology.
Unfortunately, this solution is specific to Dirac spinors and is not applicable to Weyl spinors.

More exactly, a solution to this problem may be obtained by taking the following steps:

(i) Introduce an operation, which maps
{\cal E}^{S}_{M}
to a new space of kets such that complex conjugation is involved.

(ii) Map the above-obtained space to a space of spinor bras for scalar product,
such that scalar products exist between the obtained bras and those kets in
{\cal E}^{S}_{M}
.

(iii) Construct a space dual to
{\cal E}_{M}
, by taking the direct product of the space obtained in “(ii)”
and the ordinary dual momentum-state space.

Exact ways of taking the steps will be discussed in detail in later sections,
e.g., in Sec.
IV.3
for basic modes.

6
6

6

The solution to be given is related to the so-called chiral representation of
\gamma^{\mu}
matrices
[cf. Eq.(
C.1
)].

III.3
Basic assumptions for fundamental processes

In this section, we give the rest three BAs, which concern fundamental processes.
These processes determine the time evolution of modes.
We assume that a continuous time parameter, indicated by
t
, may be used when discussing changes of mode states.

Before discussing the BAs, we need to introduce several concepts.
One concept is
antimode
.
By definition, two modes are said to be the antimode of each other,
if they have the same number of layers and spinor spaces of their corresponding layers, from top to bottom,
have the relationship of complex conjugation.
For example,
b
-mode and
\overline{b}
-mode are the antimode of each other.
Another concept is
VF pair
, refering to a pair of fermionic modes,
which are the antimode of each other
and possess opposite four-momentum
p^{\mu}=(p^{0},{\bf p})
and opposite angular momentum.

The third BA (BA3) is about the types of fundamental process.
Besides free process, we assume two other fundamental processes.
One is
VF process
, in which a VF pair emerges from or vanishes into the vacuum.
The other is
fundamental interaction process
(FIP), which has two types indicated as FIP-
1
and FIP-
2
.
In an FIP-
1
, two fermionic modes, which have a same number of layers
and whose top-layer spinor spaces are the complex conjugate of each other, change to one bosonic mode
subject to conservation of three-momentum.
The reverse of an FIP-
1
is an FIP-
2
.
Now, we state BA3 as follows.

•

BA3
. Modes may undergo three types of
fundamental processes
: free process,
VF process, and FIP.

For each fundamental process, an operator to be called
H
-operator
is of importance.
It maps the state space of the incoming mode(s) of this process to that of the outgoing mode(s).
We use
H_{\rm FP}
, with FP=free, VF, and FIP, to indicate
H
-operators of
free process, VF process, and FIP, respectively.
And, we define
p^{0}
as the expectation value of
H_{\rm free}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle p^{0}:=\text{expectation value of $H_{\rm free}$ for $|{\bf p}\rangle$}.

(9)

The fourth BA (BA4) is about a time feature of VF.
In fact, experimentally, no finite time scale has ever been observed for VF.
This suggests that a VF pair may exist instantly only;
in other words, its existence is associated with one instant
t
only.
We assume that this is related to the negative-
p^{0}
fermionic mode in the VF pair.

•

BA4
. A fermionic mode with a negative
p^{0}
may exist instantly only.

Since an FIP may contain a negative-
p^{0}
fermionic mode, too,
BA4 implies that both VF process and FIP should happen instantly.

Finally, we discuss the fifth BA (BA5), which is for the equation of time evolution.
Due to BA4, such an equation should be for states
that do not contain any negative-
p^{0}
fermionic mode.
We call a process a
basic positive-
p^{0}
interaction process
(BPIP),
if it is a combination of one FIP and some VF pair (if needed),
such that all its incoming and outgoing fermionic modes have positive
p^{0}
.
The corresponding combination of
H_{\rm FIP}
and
H_{\rm VF}

is called the
H
-operator of the BPIP, indicated as
H_{\rm BPIP}
.
Now, we are ready to introduce BA5.

•

BA5
. The time evolution of a state vector
|\Psi\rangle
,
which do not contain any negative-
p^{0}
fermionic mode, obeys the following Schrödinger-type equation,

\displaystyle i\frac{d|\Psi\rangle}{dt}=\left(H_{0}+H_{\rm int}\right)|\Psi\rangle.

(10)

On the right-hand side (rhs) of Eq.(
10
),
the time evolution operator, namely
\left(H_{0}+H_{\rm int}\right)
, is given by

\displaystyle H_{0}={\sum_{M}}^{\prime}H_{\rm free},

(11a)

\displaystyle H_{\rm int}=\sum_{\text{BPIP}}H_{\rm BPIP},

(11b)

where the prime on the rhs of (
11a
) means that
p^{0}>0
for fermionic modes
M
.

IV
Basic modes

In this section, we discuss properties of basic modes.
Specifically, we discuss their helicity states in Sec.
IV.1
,
a property of antimodes in Sec.
IV.2
, and
inner products in Sec.
IV.3
.

IV.1
Helicity states

For a given momentum
{\bf p}
, the helicity operators for
b
-mode and
\overline{b}
-mode,
indicated as
H^{\rm cy}_{b}
and
H^{\rm cy}_{\overline{b}}
, respectively, are written as

\displaystyle H^{\rm cy}_{b}=\frac{{\bf s}_{b}\cdot{\bf p}}{|{\bf p}|}\quad\&\quad H^{\rm cy}_{\overline{b}}=\frac{{\bf s}_{\overline{b}}\cdot{\bf p}}{|{\bf p}|}.

(12)

Here,
{\bf s}_{b}
and
{\bf s}_{\overline{b}}
represent the (spinor) angular momentum operators
of the two modes, respectively, with
{\bf s}_{b}\equiv(s_{b}^{1},s_{b}^{2},s_{b}^{3})
and similar for
{\bf s}_{\overline{b}}
.
(See Appendix
C.1
for detailed discussions about
{\bf s}_{b}
and
{\bf s}_{\overline{b}}
.)
These two operators have the relationship of negative complex conjugate [see Eq.(
315
)], i.e.,

\displaystyle{\bf s}_{\overline{b}}=-{\overline{{\bf s}}}_{b}.

(13)

We recall that
\overline{{\bf s}}_{b}\equiv({\bf s}_{b})^{*}
by the convention of overline implying
complex conjugation (Sec.
II
).

We use
|w^{r}({\bf p})\rangle
to indicate helicity states of a
b
-mode, with helicities
h^{r}_{b}

labelled by an index
r
,

\displaystyle H^{\rm cy}_{b}|w^{r}({\bf p})\rangle=h^{r}_{b}|w^{r}({\bf p})\rangle.

(14)

Equation (
13
) implies that helicity states of an
\overline{b}
-mode are given by
complex conjugates of
|w^{r}({\bf p})\rangle
, namely, by
|\overline{w}^{r}({\bf p})\rangle
,

\displaystyle H^{\rm cy}_{\overline{b}}|\overline{w}^{r}({\bf p})\rangle=h^{r}_{\overline{b}}|\overline{w}^{r}({\bf p})\rangle.

(15)

Detailed discussions on properties of these helicity states are given in Appendix
C.2
;
in particular,

\displaystyle h^{r}_{b}=\frac{(-1)^{r}}{2},\qquad h^{r}_{\overline{b}}=\frac{(-1)^{r+1}}{2},

(16)

where
r=0,1
(mod
2
), and

\displaystyle\langle\langle w^{r}({\bf p})|w^{s}({\bf p})\rangle=\epsilon^{rs},

(17)

where
\epsilon^{rs}
has the same matrix expression as
\epsilon^{AB}
.
Further introducing
\epsilon_{rs}
with the same matrix form,
one may use these two symbols to raise and lower the helicity index
r
, e.g.,

\displaystyle|w_{r}({\bf p})\rangle=|w^{s}({\bf p})\rangle\epsilon_{sr},\quad|w^{r}({\bf p})\rangle=\epsilon^{rs}|w_{s}({\bf p})\rangle.

(18)

From Eq.(
18
), it is seen that

\displaystyle|w_{0}({\bf p})\rangle=-|w^{1}({\bf p})\rangle,\quad|w_{1}({\bf p})\rangle=|w^{0}({\bf p})\rangle,

(19)

and, hence, the two sets of
\{|w^{r}({\bf p})\rangle\}
and
\{|w_{r}({\bf p})\rangle\}
give equivalent bases in
the space
{\mathscr{W}}
.

IV.2
Antimode-counterpart states

As discussed in Sec.
III.2
, for the purpose of getting a space
which is dual to
{\cal E}_{M}
and useful in the construction of inner product,
one needs a type of spinor bra, whose expansion coefficients are the
complex conjugates of those of the corresponding ket.
The operation of complex conjugation, though giving the wanted change of expansion coefficients,
is not capable of completing this task by itself, because for example it relates
a ket basis of
\{|S_{A}\rangle\}\in{\mathscr{W}}

to a bra basis of
\{\langle\overline{S}_{A^{\prime}}|\}
, between which no scalar product may be formed.

To complete the above-mentioned task, an additional operation is needed,
which transfers ket bases to their complex-conjugate space, meanwhile, leaves the expansion coefficients unchanged.
Such an operation is basis-dependent and, hence,
its introduction should be based on physical consideration.
It seems that the most natural way is to make use of helicity states.
More exactly, we observe that a
b
-mode and its antimode (
\overline{b}
-mode) may share a same value of helicity.
Based on this, we introduce a concept of antimode-counterpart state:
For a state of an arbitrary mode with a given momentum and a given helicity,
its
antimode-counterpart state
refers to a state of its antimode,
which possesses the same momentum and the same helicity.

Let us discuss the antimode-counterpart state of a
b
-mode state
|w^{r}({\bf p})\rangle
,
indicated as
|{w^{r}}({\bf p})\rangle_{\rm ac}
with the subscript “ac” standing for “antimode-counterpart state”.
From Eqs.(
13
) and (
16
), one sees that
|{w^{r}}({\bf p})\rangle_{\rm ac}
should be
given by
|\overline{w}^{r+1}({\bf p})\rangle
, apart from a phase factor.
Since a major goal is to construct inner product, the phase factor is of importance.
To determine it, we note that the helicity of
|\overline{w}^{r+1}({\bf p})\rangle
is equal to
that of
|\overline{w}_{r}({\bf p})\rangle
according to Eq.(
19
);
meanwhile, from
|{w^{r}}({\bf p})\rangle
to
|\overline{w}_{r}({\bf p})\rangle
,
the position of
r
is directly moved by
the most important symbol in the theory of spinors, i.e., by the
\epsilon
-symbol.

Thus, within the framework supplied by the theory of spinors, the simplest
and also most natural choice of
|{w^{r}}({\bf p})\rangle_{\rm ac}
is
|\overline{w}_{r}({\bf p})\rangle
.
Similarly, from
|w_{r}({\bf p})\rangle
, one gets an antimode-counterpart state as
|\overline{w}^{r}({\bf p})\rangle
.
It turns out that these two ways of getting antimode-counterpart state are not equivalent.
In fact, they lead to different inner products [see Eq.(
24
) to be given below].
For this reason, we introduce a label
\varrho=\pm
to make a distinction and write, by definition,

\displaystyle|{w^{r}_{\varrho}}({\bf p})\rangle_{\rm ac}:=|\overline{w}_{r\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle\qquad\text{for $\varrho=+$, }

(20a)

\displaystyle|w_{r\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle_{\rm ac}:=|\overline{w}^{r}_{\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle\qquad\text{for $\varrho=-$. }

(20b)

Direct derivation shows that [see Eq.(
332
)]

\displaystyle|w_{r\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle_{\rm ac}=-|\overline{w}^{r}_{\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle\qquad\text{for $\varrho=+$, }

(21a)

\displaystyle|w^{r}_{\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle_{\rm ac}=-|\overline{w}_{r\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle\qquad\text{for $\varrho=-$. }

(21b)

The label
\varrho
does not obey the summation convention
and there is no difference between
\varrho
in the upper and lower positions.

IV.3
Dual state space

In this section, we construct spaces dual to
{\cal E}_{b}
and
{\cal E}_{\overline{b}}
, and use them to
get products like inner product.
To this end, we make use of an operation to be called ACC operation as defined below.

•

An
ACC operation
on a state first changes the state to
its antimode-counterpart state, then, takes complex conjugation.

Note that the operation of taking antimode-counterpart state is applied to helicity states only
and does not influence expansion coefficients on helicity states.

We use
|{w^{r}}({\bf p})\rangle_{\rm ACC}
to indicate the result of an ACC operation on a state
|{w^{r}}({\bf p})\rangle
,
and similar for other spinors.
Making use of Eq.(
20
), one finds the following results of ACC operation on helicity states
(Appendix
C.3
),

\displaystyle|{w^{r}_{\varrho}}({\bf p})\rangle_{\rm ACC}

\displaystyle=\varrho|w_{r\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle,

(22a)

\displaystyle|{w_{r\varrho}}({\bf p})\rangle_{\rm ACC}

\displaystyle=-\varrho|w^{r}_{\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle.

(22b)

It is of interest to note that, within an ACC operation,
taking antimode-counterpart state and taking complex conjugation are in fact commutable
(Appendix
C.3
).

Expanding the above-obtained ACC kets on the basis of
|S^{A}\rangle
and making use of Eq.(
3
)
to change kets to spinor bras for scalar product, one gets bras which we call
ACC-bras
.
The ACC-bras involve complex conjugation and, hence, we use to
\langle\cdot|
to indicate them;
in addition, we use a hat to indicate that ACC operation has been used to get the bras.
Thus, according to Eq.(
22
), we get the following ACC-bras of helicity states,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{w^{r}_{\varrho}}({\bf p})|

\displaystyle=\varrho\langle w_{r\varrho}({\bf p})|,

(23a)

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{w_{r\varrho}}({\bf p})|

\displaystyle=-\varrho\langle w^{r}_{\varrho}({\bf p})|.

(23b)

Then, making use of Eqs.(
17
) and Eq.(
22
) and noting Eq.(
214
),
it is easy to check that

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{w^{r}_{\varrho}}({\bf p})|{w^{s}_{\varrho}}({\bf p})\rangle=\varrho\ \delta^{rs},

(24a)

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{w_{r\varrho}}({\bf p})|{w_{s\varrho}}({\bf p})\rangle=\varrho\ \delta_{rs}.

(24b)

For an arbitrary spinor
|\phi\rangle
in the space
{\mathscr{W}}
, written as
|\phi\rangle=c_{r}|{w^{r}_{\varrho}}({\bf p})\rangle
,
the ACC operation gives

\displaystyle|{\phi}\rangle_{\rm ACC}

\displaystyle:=\overline{|\phi\rangle_{\rm ac}}\equiv|\overline{\phi}\rangle_{\rm ac}=\overline{c}_{r}|{w^{r}}({\bf p})\rangle_{\rm ACC},

(25)

with
\overline{c}_{r}\equiv c^{*}_{r}
.
Then, one has

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\phi}|

\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{w^{r}}({\bf p})|\overline{c}_{r}.

(26)

We call the scalar product of an ACC-bra and a ket, say,
\langle\widehat{\phi}|\phi^{\prime}\rangle
, an
ACC-scalar product
.
Making use of Eq.(
24
), one finds that the
ACC-scalar product is an inner product for
\varrho=+
,
while, it is a negative inner product for
\varrho=-
.
(See Appendix
D.1
for a proof in a generic case.)

Thus, with respect to helicity states, we have constructed a space dual to
{\cal E}^{S}_{b}
,
which is spanned by ACC-bras obtained from kets in
{\cal E}^{S}_{b}
by the ACC operation.
We call such a dual space an
ACC-dual space
.
This space satisfies requirements given at the first and second steps discussed in Sec.
III.2
.

It is easy to see that the direct product of the ACC-dual space and the ordinary dual momentum-state space
gives a space, which is dual to the state space
{\cal E}_{b}
of
b
-mode.
For the sake of brevity in presentation, we introduce the notation of
\widehat{\langle{\bf p}|}
and set it as

\displaystyle\widehat{\langle{\bf p}|}:=\langle{\bf p}|.

(27)

Then, for an arbitrary vector

|\psi\rangle=\int d\widetilde{p}\ c_{{\bf p}r}|{\bf p}\rangle|{w^{r}_{\varrho}}({\bf p})\rangle
with coefficients
c_{{\bf p}r}
, one gets that

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\psi}|

\displaystyle:=\int d\widetilde{p}\ \overline{c}_{{\bf p}r}\langle{\bf p}|\langle\widehat{w^{r}}({\bf p})|.

(28)

When momentum states are included, we speak of ACC-bra in the sense of Eq.(
28
).

It is easy to check that the above discussions are also valid for
\overline{b}
-mode.
In particular, Eqs.(
20
)-(
24
) are valid under complex conjugation.

Finally, since different signs of
\varrho
correspond to different types of inner product,
we assume that modes with different signs of
\varrho
behave as
different species
of mode.
For this reason, states with different signs of
\varrho
are always orthogonal.

V
Unit modes besides basic modes

In this section, we discuss unit modes besides basic modes,
whose spinor spaces are obtained from direct products of some of
{\mathscr{W}}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
.
From the angle of spinor, such a unit mode may be regarded as being constructed from some basic modes.
We assume that, in this understanding, the “ingredient” basic modes share a common momentum.
Then, for “ingredient” basic modes within a unit mode, the fermionic relationship in Eq.(
8a
) implies that

\displaystyle|S_{A}\rangle|\overline{S}_{B^{\prime}}\rangle=-|\overline{S}_{B^{\prime}}\rangle|S_{A}\rangle,

(29a)

\displaystyle|S_{A}\rangle|S_{B}\rangle=-|S_{B}\rangle|S_{A}\rangle,

(29b)

\displaystyle|\overline{S}_{A^{\prime}}\rangle|\overline{S}_{B^{\prime}}\rangle=-|\overline{S}_{B^{\prime}}\rangle|\overline{S}_{A^{\prime}}\rangle.

(29c)

V.1
z
-mode

In this section, we discuss a unit mode, whose spinor space is obtained from
the direct product space of
{\mathscr{W}}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
.
As is known in the spinor theory,
{\mathscr{W}}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
is isomorphic to a four-component-vector space,
which we denote by
{\mathscr{V}}
.
We call this mode
z
-mode
.
Clearly, the space
{\mathscr{V}}
coincides with its complex conjugate, i.e.,
\overline{{\mathscr{V}}}={\mathscr{V}}
,
and hence
z
-mode is itself’s antimode.

For brevity, sometimes we write
|S_{A}\rangle|\overline{S}_{B^{\prime}}\rangle
as
|S_{AB^{\prime}}\rangle

and
\langle\langle\overline{S}_{B^{\prime}}|\langle\langle S_{A}|
as
\langle\langle S_{B^{\prime}A}|
.
The space
{\mathscr{V}}
is spanned by four vectors
|T^{\mu}\rangle
of
\mu=0,1,2,3
, which
are linked to spinors in
{\mathscr{W}}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
through
an
EW-symbol operator

\sigma
defined by

\sigma:=\sigma^{\mu AB^{\prime}}|T_{\mu}\rangle\langle\langle S_{B^{\prime}A}|,

(30)

where
\sigma^{\mu AB^{\prime}}
indicate the so-called Enfeld-van der Waerden symbols, in short
EW-symbols

(see also Appendix
A.3
).
Scalar products of these vectors are written as
\langle\langle T^{\mu}|T^{\nu}\rangle=g^{\mu\nu}
[see Eq.(
260
)],
where
g^{\mu\nu}
is the Minkovski’s metric in Eq.(
A.2
).

We use
{\bf J}_{\mathscr{V}}
to denote the angular-momentum operator that acts on the space
{\mathscr{V}}
.
It should come from the sum of
{\bf s}_{b}
and
{\bf s}_{\overline{b}}
,
namely,
({\bf s}_{b}I_{\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}}+{\bf s}_{\overline{b}}I_{{\mathscr{W}}})
, where
I_{\mathscr{W}}
and
I_{\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}}

are identity operators in
{\mathscr{W}}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
[see Eqs.(
217
) and (
221
)], respectively.
Since
\sigma
maps
{\mathscr{W}}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
to
{\mathscr{V}}

and the reverse map is written as
\sigma^{-1}=\sigma^{T}
[Eq.(
279
)],
{\bf J}_{\mathscr{V}}
has the following form,

\displaystyle{\bf J}_{\mathscr{V}}\equiv({\bf J}_{\mathscr{V}})^{\mu\nu}|T_{\mu}\rangle\langle\langle T_{\nu}|=\sigma\left({\bf s}_{b}I_{\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}}+{\bf s}_{\overline{b}}I_{{\mathscr{W}}}\right)\sigma^{T}.

(31)

Making use of the explicit expressions of the EW-symbols in Eq.(
A.2
), one finds that (Appendix
E.1
)

\displaystyle(J^{3}_{\mathscr{V}})^{\mu\nu}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&0&0&0\\
0&0&i&0\\
0&-i&0&0\\
0&0&0&0\end{array}\right).

We use
|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle
of
\lambda=0,1,2,3

to denote helicity states of a
z
-mode with a momentum
{\bf k}
and helicities
h^{\lambda}_{z}
,

\displaystyle H^{\rm cy}_{z}|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle=h^{\lambda}_{z}|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle,

(36)

where
H^{\rm cy}_{z}={\bf J}_{\mathscr{V}}\cdot{\bf k}/|{\bf k}|
.
For
{\bf k}_{0}=(0,0,|{\bf k}|)
, one has

\displaystyle\left[\left(J^{3}_{\mathscr{V}}\right)^{\mu\nu}|T_{\mu}\rangle\langle\langle T_{\nu}|\right]|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k}_{0})\rangle=h^{\lambda}_{z}|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k}_{0})\rangle.

(37)

Using Eq.(
V.1
), one finds the following eigenstates,

\displaystyle|\varepsilon^{0}({\bf k}_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=|T^{0}\rangle,

(38a)

\displaystyle|\varepsilon^{1}({\bf k}_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|T^{1}\rangle+i|T^{2}\rangle),

(38b)

\displaystyle|\varepsilon^{2}({\bf k}_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|T^{1}\rangle-i|T^{2}\rangle),

(38c)

\displaystyle|\varepsilon^{3}({\bf k}_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=|T^{3}\rangle,

(38d)

with helicities

\displaystyle h^{1}_{z}=1,\quad h^{2}_{z}=-1,\quad h^{0}_{z}=h^{3}_{z}=0.

(39)

The helicity states
|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle
are then obtained by
the
SO(3)
rotation that transforms the direction of
{\bf k}_{0}
to that of
{\bf k}
.

Generically, one may consider a vector
|\psi\rangle
,
which is an eigenstate of both
s_{b}^{k}I_{\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}}
and
s_{\overline{b}}^{k}I_{{\mathscr{W}}}
,
with eigenvalues of
a
and
b
, respectively, i.e.,

\displaystyle s^{k}_{b}I_{\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}}|\psi\rangle=a|\psi\rangle,\quad s^{k}_{\overline{b}}I_{{\mathscr{W}}}|\psi\rangle=b|\psi\rangle.

(40)

One easily checks that
|\Psi\rangle=\sigma|\psi\rangle
is an eigenstate of
J^{k}_{\mathscr{V}}
with an eigenvalue
(a+b)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle J^{k}_{\mathscr{V}}|\Psi\rangle=(a+b)|\Psi\rangle.

(41)

The helicity states
|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k}_{0})\rangle
in Eq.(
38
) may also be written in this form.
Indeed, from Eqs.(
38
) and (
267
), one finds that

\displaystyle|\varepsilon^{0}({\bf k}_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma(|S^{11^{\prime}}\rangle+|S^{00^{\prime}}\rangle),

(42a)

\displaystyle|\varepsilon^{1}({\bf k}_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=-{\sqrt{2}}\sigma|S^{10^{\prime}}\rangle,

(42b)

\displaystyle|\varepsilon^{2}({\bf k}_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=-{\sqrt{2}}\sigma|S^{01^{\prime}}\rangle,

(42c)

\displaystyle|\varepsilon^{3}({\bf k}_{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sigma(|S^{11^{\prime}}\rangle-|S^{00^{\prime}}\rangle);

(42d)

then, making use of Eqs.(
13
) and (
C.2
), one checks that

|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k}_{0})\rangle
may also be written in the form of
|\Psi\rangle=\sigma|\psi\rangle
.

Finally, we discuss ACC-bras.
Since the antimode of a
z
-mode is itself,
the antimode-counterpart state of
|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle
is just itself, i.e.,

|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle_{\rm ac}=|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle
.
Then, one finds that

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\varepsilon^{\lambda}}({\bf k})|=\langle\langle\overline{\varepsilon}^{\lambda}({\bf k})|.

(43)

Due to the relation of
|\overline{T}^{\mu}\rangle=-|T^{\mu}\rangle
in Eq.(
272
), Eq.(
38
) implies that

\displaystyle|\overline{\varepsilon}^{0}({\bf k})\rangle=-|\varepsilon^{0}({\bf k})\rangle,\quad|\overline{\varepsilon}^{3}({\bf k})\rangle=-|\varepsilon^{3}({\bf k})\rangle,

(44a)

\displaystyle|\overline{\varepsilon}^{1}({\bf k})\rangle=-|\varepsilon^{2}({\bf k})\rangle,\quad|\overline{\varepsilon}^{2}({\bf k})\rangle=-|\varepsilon^{1}({\bf k})\rangle.

(44b)

We use
\widehat{g}^{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}
to indicate ACC-scalar products of the
|\varepsilon\rangle
-vectors, i.e.,

\displaystyle\widehat{g}^{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}:=\langle\widehat{\varepsilon^{\lambda}}({\bf k})|\varepsilon^{\lambda^{\prime}}({\bf k})\rangle.

(45)

Making use of Eq.(
272
), it is easy to check that

\displaystyle\widehat{g}^{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}=-g^{\mu\mu^{\prime}}\quad\text{with $\lambda=\mu,\lambda^{\prime}=\mu^{\prime}$}.

(46)

The direct product of the ACC-bra space spanned by
\langle\widehat{\varepsilon^{\lambda}}({\bf k})|

and the space spanned by
\langle{\bf k}|
gives a space dual to
{\cal E}_{z}
.

V.2
s-mode,
\overline{s}
-mode, and others

In this section, we first discuss unit modes, whose spinor spaces, denoted by
{\mathscr{S}}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
,
are related to the two product spaces of
{\mathscr{W}}\otimes{\mathscr{W}}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
, respectively.
Then, we argue that there is no further unit mode of interest physically.

We call modes, whose spinor spaces are
{\mathscr{S}}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
,
s
-mode
and
\overline{s}
-mode
, respectively.
As a consequence of Eq.(
29b
),

|S_{0}\rangle|S_{0}\rangle=|S_{1}\rangle|S_{1}\rangle=0
,
|S_{1}\rangle|S_{0}\rangle=-|S_{0}\rangle|S_{1}\rangle
,
and similar for their complex conjugates.
This implies that, say,
{\mathscr{S}}
may not be isomorphic to
{\mathscr{W}}\otimes{\mathscr{W}}
.
In fact, both
{\mathscr{S}}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
are one-dimensional
SL(2,C)
-scalar spaces (see Appendix
E.2
).
We use
|s\rangle
and
|\overline{s}\rangle
to denote basis scalars in
{\mathscr{S}}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
, respectively.

In fact, the physical requirement of fermionicness, from which Eq.(
29
) is gotten,
lies outside the mathematical theory of spinors.
It may render unusual properties to
s(\overline{s})
-mode.
In particular, a scalar space like
{\mathscr{S}}
may not accommodate any nontrivial angular momentum operator,
while, the space
{\mathscr{W}}^{(1)}\otimes{\mathscr{W}}^{(2)}
has a nontrivial angular momentum operator
written as
\left({\bf s}^{(1)}_{b}I^{(2)}_{{\mathscr{W}}}+{\bf s}^{(1)}_{b}I^{(2)}_{{\mathscr{W}}}\right)
.
For this reason, at the present stage, it is unclear what type of angular-momentum property should be
assigned to
s(\overline{s})
-mode.
Anyway, both
s
-mode and
\overline{s}
-mode should be bosonic.

In discussions to be given below,
we assume that physical impact of the above-discussed indefiniteness of
s(\overline{s})
-mode
should be reduced as much as possible.
More exactly, we assume that
(i)
s
-mode and
\overline{s}
-mode may not exist independently,
(ii) they do not need to satisfy the requirement of identical layer-helicity stated in BA2,
and (iii) they have vanishing ACC-scalar products, i.e.,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{s}|s\rangle=\langle\widehat{\overline{s}}|\overline{s}\rangle=0.

(47)

Due to the vanishing ACC-scalar products in Eq.(
47
),

s(\overline{s})
-mode may not be detected directly by experiments.

Finally, we argue that there is no further unit mode of interest physically.
Clearly, due to the relation in Eq.(
29b
),
no unit mode may be constructed from either three
b
-modes, or from three
\overline{b}
-modes.
Moreover, a product of
|S_{A}\rangle|S_{B}\rangle|\overline{S}_{C^{\prime}}\rangle
is not of interest physically,
because it must be proportional to
|s\rangle|\overline{S}_{C^{\prime}}\rangle
,
which transforms likes a Weyl spinor
|\overline{S}_{C^{\prime}}\rangle
, but,
always has a vanishing ACC-scalar product due to Eq.(
47
).
By similar arguments, products of more than three of
{\mathscr{W}}
and
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
are not of interest, either.

VI
Multilayer modes

In this section, we discuss multi-layer modes.
Specifically, we give some preliminary discussions in Sec.
VI.1
,
discuss fermionic and bosonic modes in Sec.
VI.2
and Sec.
VI.3
,
respectively, and give identity operators for states of modes in Sec.
VI.4
.
According to previous discussions, there are only five species of unit mode,
whose spinor states may be used as mode layers,
i.e.,
b
-mode,
\overline{b}
-mode,
z
-mode,
s
-mode, and
\overline{s}
-mode;
we indicate the corresponding layers as

b
-layer,
\overline{b}
-layer,
z
-layer,
s
-layer, and
\overline{s}
-layer, respectively.

VI.1
Preliminary discussions

In this section, we first discuss a notation to be used,
then, give a formal discussion for layer states,
and, finally, generalize Eq.(
13
) to a generic mode.

1.
Notation of
\alpha
.

When writing helicity states of multilayer modes, it is inconvenient to use the index
r
.
For example, for a fermionic mode consisting of one
b
-layer and one
\overline{b}
-layer,
if the
b
-layer lies in a state
|w^{r}_{\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle
,
then, the
\overline{b}
-layer should lie in a state
|\overline{w}_{r,\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle
with the label
r
in the lower position,
such that the two layers possess the same helicity obeying BA2 [cf. Eqs.(
16
) and (
18
)].
The inconvenience lies in that
the helicity label
r
for the whole mode does not have a definite position.
In addition, the label
\varrho
should also be indicated when writing a state of a fermionic mode.

To solve the above problems,
we introduce an index
\alpha
,
\alpha:=(r,\varrho)
, and use it in the following way
of indexing helicity states,

\displaystyle|u^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle:=|w^{r}_{\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle,

(48a)

\displaystyle|v^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle:=|w_{r\varrho}({\bf p})\rangle.

(48b)

Clearly, for the mode in the above-discussed example, the label
\alpha
has a definite (upper) position.

Two remarks:
(i)
|u^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
and
|\overline{v}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle

have the same helicity
,
in contrast to that
|w^{r}({\bf p})\rangle
and
|\overline{w}^{r}({\bf p})\rangle
have opposite helicities.
And, (ii) since the label
\varrho
does not obey the summation convention,
repeated
\alpha
implies a summation only over
r\in\alpha
, not including
\varrho
.

From Eqs.(
23
) and (
48
), one sees that

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{u}^{\alpha}({\bf p})|=\varrho(\alpha)\langle v^{\alpha}({\bf p})|,

(49a)

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{v}^{\alpha}({\bf p})|=-\varrho(\alpha)\langle u^{\alpha}({\bf p})|,

(49b)

where
\varrho(\alpha)
indicates the sign
\varrho
in
\alpha
.
Making use of Eqs.(
48
), (
17
), and (
214
), it is direct to check that

\displaystyle\langle v^{\alpha}({\bf p})|u^{\beta}({\bf p})\rangle=-\langle u^{\beta}({\bf p})|v^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle=\delta^{\alpha\beta},

(50)

where
\beta=(s,\varrho^{\prime})

and
\delta^{\alpha\beta}:=\delta^{rs}\delta^{\varrho\varrho^{\prime}}
, as well as that

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{u}^{\alpha}({\bf p})|{u^{\beta}({\bf p})}\rangle=\langle\widehat{v}^{\alpha}({\bf p})|{v^{\beta}}({\bf p})\rangle=\varrho(\alpha)\delta^{\alpha\beta}.

(51)

2.
Layer states
.

W use
e^{i\vartheta_{m}^{M}}|{\mathcal{L}}_{m}^{M}\rangle
to indicate the spinor state of the
m
-th layer of a mode
M
.
We call
\vartheta_{m}^{M}
the
layer phase
and call
|{\mathcal{L}}_{m}^{M}\rangle
the
layer spinor
.
For a given mode, BA2 requires that
|{\mathcal{L}}_{m}^{M}\rangle
should possess the same helicity for all
m
.

3.
An angular-momentum relation for generic antimodes
.

For a generic mode
M
,
we use
{\cal E}_{M}^{S-\text{ext}}
to indicate the space, which is spanned by
basis spinors like those of
{\cal E}^{S}_{M}
, but, not obeying the requirement of identical layer helicity imposed by BA2.
Clearly, this space
{\cal E}_{M}^{S-\text{ext}}
is just the direct sum of all those spaces,
each corresponding to one layer of
M
.
We use
{\bf S}_{M}\equiv(S^{1}_{M},S^{2}_{M},S_{M}^{3})

to indicate the spinor angular-momentum operator on the space
{\cal E}_{M}^{S-\text{ext}}
.
Similarly, for the antimode
\overline{M}
, one has a space
{\cal E}_{\overline{M}}^{S-\text{ext}}

and an operator
{\bf S}_{\overline{M}}
.
A key point is that
{\cal E}_{\overline{M}}^{S-\text{ext}}
is the complex conjugate of
{\cal E}_{M}^{S-\text{ext}}
.

Below, we show that Eq.(
13
) is valid in this generic case.
Consider an arbitrary element of the group
SU(2)
,
which is written as
{\cal R}_{M}
when acting on the spinor space
{\cal E}_{M}^{S-\text{ext}}
,
while, is written as
{\mathcal{R}}_{\overline{M}}
for
{\cal E}_{\overline{M}}^{S-\text{ext}}
.
As is well known,
{\mathcal{R}}_{M}
is characterized by three real parameters
\theta_{k}

and is written as

\displaystyle{\cal R}_{M}=e^{-i\theta_{k}S_{M}^{k}}.

(52)

For the operator
{\mathcal{R}}_{\overline{M}}
, on one hand, it should be given by the complex conjugate of
{\mathcal{R}}_{M}
,
i.e.,
{\mathcal{R}}_{\overline{M}}=e^{i\theta_{k}{\overline{S}}^{k}_{M}}
;
on the other hand, it should be characterized by the same parameters
\theta_{k}

with respect to the operator
{\bf S}_{\overline{M}}
,
that is, written as
{\mathcal{R}}_{\overline{M}}=e^{-i\theta_{k}S_{\overline{M}}^{k}}
.
Then, one sees that the angular-momentum operators of these two modes should satisfy
an equation like Eq.(
13
), i.e.,

\displaystyle{\bf S}_{\overline{M}}=-\overline{{\bf S}}_{M}.

(53)

VI.2
Fermionic modes

In this section, we discuss states of multilayer fermionic modes.
Layers of these modes are either
b
-layer or
\overline{b}
-layer.

We use
f
to indicate a fermionic mode, whose first layer lies in a space
{\mathscr{W}}
.
Under a given momentum
{\bf p}
and a given label
\alpha
, we write the top-layer’s spinor as
|u^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
.
Then, the state of the mode, denoted by
|f^{\alpha}_{{\bf p}}\rangle
, is written as

\displaystyle|f^{\alpha}_{{\bf p}}\rangle=|{\bf p}\rangle|\mathcal{S}_{f}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle,

(54)

where
|\mathcal{S}_{f}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
indicates its spinor part,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{S}_{f}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{f}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}e^{i\vartheta_{1}^{f}}|u^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle\\
e^{i\vartheta_{2}^{f}}|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{2}\rangle\\
\vdots\\
e^{i\vartheta_{n_{f}}^{f}}|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{n_{f}}\rangle\end{array}\right)

with
n_{f}
the number of layers of
f
.
According to BA2, layer spinors of other layers with
{\mathscr{W}}

should be written as
|u^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
, too, while, those with
\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
as
|\overline{v}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{m}\rangle=|u^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle\ \text{or}\ |\overline{v}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle\quad(|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{1}\rangle=|u^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle).

(59)

The corresponding state of the antimode
\overline{f}
is written as

|\overline{f}^{\alpha}_{{\bf p}}\rangle=|{\bf p}\rangle|\mathcal{S}_{\overline{f}}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
.
For
|\mathcal{S}_{\overline{f}}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
and
|\mathcal{S}_{f}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
to have the same helicity,
one needs to set the first layer spinor of
|\mathcal{S}_{\overline{f}}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
as
|\overline{v}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
.
Thus, we write

\displaystyle|\mathcal{S}_{\overline{f}}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{f}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}e^{i\vartheta_{1}^{\overline{f}}}|\overline{v}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle\\
e^{i\vartheta_{2}^{\overline{f}}}|{\mathcal{L}}^{\overline{f}}_{2}\rangle\\
\vdots\\
e^{i\vartheta_{n_{f}}^{\overline{f}}}|{\mathcal{L}}^{\overline{f}}_{n_{f}}\rangle\end{array}\right),

where
|{\mathcal{L}}^{\overline{f}}_{m}\rangle=|\overline{v}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle\ \text{or}\ |u^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
.

Now, we discuss ACC-bras of the above states.
Note that the previously given definitions of antimode-counterpart state
and of ACC operation are valid for an arbitrary mode
M
.
When taking the antimode-counterpart state of
|\mathcal{S}_{f}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle
,
the layer phases should be changed to those of its antimode
\overline{f}
.
This gives that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{S}_{f}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle_{\rm ac}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{f}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}e^{i\vartheta_{1}^{\overline{f}}}|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{1}\rangle_{\rm ac}\\
\vdots\\
e^{i\vartheta_{n}^{\overline{f}}}|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{n_{f}}\rangle_{\rm ac}\end{array}\right).

Then, one gets that

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\mathcal{S}}^{\alpha}_{f}({\bf p})|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{f}}}\left(e^{-i\vartheta_{1}^{\overline{f}}}\langle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{1}|,e^{-i\vartheta_{2}^{\overline{f}}}\langle\widehat{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{2}|,\cdots\right).

(67)

For these states to possess the same scalar products as those in Eq.(
51
),
the two modes of
f
and
\overline{f}
must share the same layer phases, i.e.,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{m}^{f}=\vartheta_{m}^{\overline{f}}\qquad\forall m.

(68)

Then, one gets the following scalar product,

7
7

7

In the case of a two-layer mode of
\varrho=+
, with one
b
-layer and one
\overline{b}
-layer,
it is straightforward to check that this product is effectively
the same as the ordinary inner product for Dirac spinors.

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{f}^{\beta}({\bf p})|\mathcal{S}_{f}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle=\varrho(\alpha)\delta^{\beta\alpha}.

(69)

It is not difficult to see that the above results are also valid for the antimode
\overline{f}
.

To summarize, for an arbitrary fermionic mode
F
, one has

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{F}^{\beta}({\bf q})|F^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle=\varrho(\alpha)|{\bf p}|\delta^{3}({\bf p}-{\bf q})\delta^{\alpha\beta}.

(70)

This ACC-scalar product is an inner product for
\varrho=+
,
while, it is a negative inner product for
\varrho=-
(Appendix
D.1
).

VI.3
Bosonic modes

In this section, we discuss spinor states of multilayer bosonic modes,
the layers of which lie in
{\mathscr{V}}
, or
{\mathscr{S}}
, or
\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}
.

In the construction of a bosonic mode, further attention should be paid to the space
{\mathscr{V}}
.
In fact, although
{\mathscr{V}}
is its own complex conjugate,
the helicity states
|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle
of
\lambda=1,2

are not equal to their complex conjugates
|\overline{\varepsilon}^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle
[Eq.(
44
)].
We assume that these two cases should be distinguished,
more exactly, there may exist two types of four-component vector layers within one bosonic mode,
which we indicate as
z
-layer
and
\overline{z}
-layer
, respectively.

We use
|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle
to indicate the spinor state of a
z
-layer in a bosonic mode.
According to BA2, the layer spinor of an
\overline{z}
-layer in this mode
should be described by the vector
|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle
, too.
To make a distinction, we use
|\varpi^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle
to indicate the spinor state of the
\overline{z}
-layer, with

\displaystyle|\varpi^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle\equiv|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle.

(71)

One should note that
z
-layer and
\overline{z}
-layer have a relative difference only
and one needs to distinguish between them only when they appear within a same bosonic mode.
For example, there is no essential difference between the two configurations of

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle\\
|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle\end{array}\right)

and
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}|\varpi^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle\\
|\varpi^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle\end{array}\right)
.

Thus, the spinor state of a bosonic mode
|B\rangle
, whose first, second, and third layers are

z
-layer,
\overline{z}
-layer, and
s
-layer, respectively, is written as

\displaystyle|\mathcal{S}_{B}^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{V}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}e^{i\vartheta_{1}^{B}}|\varepsilon^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle\\
e^{i\vartheta_{2}^{B}}|\varpi^{\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle\\
|s\rangle\\
\vdots\end{array}\right),

where
n_{V}
indicates the number of the
z(\overline{z})
-layers.
The corresponding ACC-bra is written as

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{B}^{\lambda}}({\bf k})|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{V}}}\big(e^{-i\vartheta_{1}^{\overline{B}}}\langle\widehat{\varepsilon^{\lambda}}({\bf k})|,e^{-i\vartheta_{2}^{\overline{B}}}\langle\widehat{\varpi^{\lambda}}|,\langle\widehat{s}|,...\big).

(76)

Similarly to Eq.(
68
), one has
\vartheta_{m}^{B}=\vartheta_{m}^{\overline{B}}
for all
m
.
Then, making use of Eqs.(
45
) and (
47
), one finds that

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{B}^{\lambda}}({\bf k})|\mathcal{S}_{B}^{\lambda^{\prime}}({\bf k})\rangle=\widehat{g}^{\lambda\lambda^{\prime}}.

(77)

VI.4
Identity operators

In this section, we discuss identity operators that act on state spaces of one and two modes.
We extend usage of the index
\alpha
to a generic mode
M
, defined by

\displaystyle\alpha:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(r,\varrho)&\hbox{for a fermionic mode,}\\
\lambda&\hbox{for a bosonic mode.}\end{array}\right.

States of
M
are then written as

\displaystyle|M^{\alpha}_{{\bf p}}\rangle=|{\bf p}\rangle|\mathcal{S}_{M}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle.

(80)

From Eqs.(
7
), (
69
), and (
77
), one gets that

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{M}^{\alpha}_{{\bf p}}|M^{\beta}_{{\bf q}}\rangle=|{\bf p}|\delta^{3}({\bf p}-{\bf q})\Upsilon^{\alpha\beta},

(81)

where
\Upsilon^{\alpha\beta}
indicates a symbol defined by

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\alpha\beta}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\varrho(\alpha)\delta^{\alpha\beta}&\hbox{for fermionic modes,}\\
\widehat{g}^{\alpha\beta}&\hbox{for bosonic modes.}\end{array}\right.

It is easy to verify that
\Upsilon^{\alpha\beta}=\Upsilon^{\beta\alpha}
and

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\alpha\beta}=\Upsilon(\alpha,\alpha)\delta^{\alpha\beta},\ \ \left(\Upsilon(\alpha,\alpha)\right)^{2}=1,

(84)

where
\Upsilon(\alpha,\alpha)
indicates
\Upsilon^{\alpha\alpha}
without taking summation.
Furthermore, introducing a symbol
\Upsilon_{\alpha\beta}
,
which by definition has the same matrix as
\Upsilon^{\alpha\beta}
,
one may raise and lower the index
\alpha
.
For example,

\displaystyle|\psi_{\alpha}\rangle=\Upsilon_{\alpha\beta}|\psi^{\beta}\rangle,\quad|\psi^{\alpha}\rangle=\Upsilon^{\alpha\beta}|\psi_{\beta}\rangle.

(85)

Note that, to get the relation between a label
\alpha
in the lower position (e.g., in
|\psi_{\alpha}\rangle
)
and a helicity label
r
, one first needs to relate it to a label
\beta
in the upper position,
then, makes use of Eq.(
48
).
To avoid potential confusion, when the notation of
\alpha
is used,
we never change position of the label
r
involved.

It is easy to check that

{X_{\ldots}^{\ \ \ \alpha}\ (Y)^{\cdots}}_{\alpha}={X_{\ldots\alpha}\ (Y)^{\cdots\alpha}},

(86)

i.e., exchange of positions of repeated
\alpha
brings no change.
Moreover, using Eq.(
84
), one finds that

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\alpha}^{\ \ \beta}=\Upsilon^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta}\Upsilon_{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}=\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta},\quad\Upsilon^{\alpha}_{\ \ \beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}.

(87)

and this implies that

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{M}_{{\bf p}\alpha}|M^{\beta}_{{\bf q}}\rangle=|{\bf p}|\delta^{3}({\bf p}-{\bf q})\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}.

(88)

Making use of properties discussed above,
it is straightforward to verify that the identity operator that acts on
{\cal E}_{M}
, denoted by
I_{M}
, is written as

\displaystyle I_{M}=\int d\widetilde{p}|M^{\alpha}_{{\bf p}}\rangle\langle\widehat{M}_{{\bf p}\alpha}|.

(89)

For the space
{\cal E}_{M}\otimes{\cal E}_{M^{\prime}}
of two different modes
M
and
M^{\prime}
,
the identity operator is written as

\displaystyle I_{MM^{\prime}}=\int d\widetilde{p}d\widetilde{p}^{\prime}|M^{\alpha}_{{\bf p}}{M^{\prime}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{{\bf p}^{\prime}}\rangle\langle{\widehat{M^{\prime}}}_{{\bf p}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}\widehat{M}_{{\bf p}\alpha}|.

(90)

VII
H-operators

In this section, we discuss
H
-operators of fundamental processes,
which map the state spaces of incoming modes to those of outgoing modes in straightforward ways.
In Sec.
VII.1
, we give generic discussions.
Expressions of
H
-operators of free process and of FIP are derived in Secs.
VII.2
and
VII.3
, respectively.
In Sec.
VII.4
, we write the
H
-operator of FIP in a concise form.

H
-operators of VF and of BPIPs are discussed in Sec.
VII.5
.
Finally, field expressions of the
H
-operators of free processes and of
H_{\rm int}
are derived in Sec.
VII.6
.

VII.1
Generic form of H-operators

We use
X
to indicate the set of modes that enters into a fundamental process
and use
Y
for the outgoing set.
States of modes in the two sets are written as
|X^{\eta}_{\bf P}\rangle=|{\bf P}\rangle|\mathcal{S}_{X}^{\eta}({\bf P})\rangle

and
|Y^{\xi}_{\bf Q}\rangle=|{\bf Q}\rangle|\mathcal{S}_{Y}^{\xi}({\bf Q})\rangle
,
lying in spaces denoted by
{\cal E}_{X}
and
{\cal E}_{Y}
, respectively,
where
{\bf P}={\bf p}
for one mode and
{\bf P}=({\bf p},{\bf p}^{\prime})
for two modes, similar for
{\bf Q}
,
and
\eta
and
\xi
represent the corresponding spinor indices.
Exact meanings of
X
and
Y
are as follows.

•

Free process:
X=Y=M
.

•

VF:
X=|0\rangle
and
Y=(f,\overline{f})
for emergence of a VF pair,
where
|0\rangle
indicates the vacuum state, and the reverse is for vanishing of a VF pair.

•

FIP:
X=f\overline{f}^{\prime}
and
Y=B
for an FIP-
1
, and the reverse is for an FIP-
2
.

Thus, more exactly, we write
H_{\rm FP}
as
H^{X\to Y}_{\rm FP}
, which maps
{\cal E}_{X}
to
{\cal E}_{Y}
.

Below in this section, we discuss
H^{X\to Y}_{\rm free}
and
H^{X\to Y}_{\rm FIP}
.
To carry out the maps they describe, these two
H
-operators should be written in a form like

\displaystyle|Y^{\xi}_{\bf Q}\rangle\cdots\langle\widehat{X}^{\eta}_{\bf P}|,

(91)

where the bra is written as an ACC-bra, because we need it to annihilate a state vector in the space
{\cal E}_{X}
.
The simplest operator that includes the ACC-bra in (
91
) is the identity operator that acts on the space
{\cal E}_{X}
,
which we denote by
I_{X}
.
According to Eqs.(
89
)-(
90
), it is written as

\displaystyle I_{X}=\int d\widetilde{\bf P}|{\bf P}\rangle|\mathcal{S}_{X}^{\eta}({\bf P})\rangle\langle\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{X\eta}({\bf P})|\langle{\bf P}|.

(92)

We use
P_{Y}
to indicate the operator that includes the ket
|Y^{\xi}_{\bf Q}\rangle
in (
91
) and write

\displaystyle H^{X\to Y}_{\rm FP}=P_{Y}{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}I_{X},

(93)

where
{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}
is an operator that accomplishes the main mapping function.

For
Y
as a single mode
M
, it is natural to take
P_{Y}
as the identity operator in Eq.(
89
),
i.e.,
P_{Y}=I_{Y}
.
While, for
Y
as two fermionic modes, although the identity operator in Eq.(
90
) is a simple choice,
one may not say that it is the only natural choice,
as to be discussed later at the end of Sec.
VII.3
.

It is natural to assume that
{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}
is the product of a momentum part
denoted by
{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}_{\rm mom}
and a spinor part denoted by
{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}_{\rm sp}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}={\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}_{\rm mom}{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}_{\rm sp}.

(94)

Then, for
Y
as a single mode, we get that

\displaystyle H^{X\to Y}_{\rm FP}=\int d\widetilde{\bf P}d\widetilde{\bf Q}|{Y}^{\xi}_{{\bf Q}}\rangle\ C^{X\to Y}_{{\bf P}{\bf Q}}C^{X\to Y}_{\eta\xi}\langle{{\widehat{X}}}_{{\bf P}}^{\eta}|,

(95)

where

\displaystyle C^{X\to Y}_{{\bf P}{\bf Q}}=\langle{\bf Q}|{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}_{\rm mom}|{\bf P}\rangle,

(96a)

\displaystyle C^{X\to Y}_{\eta\xi}=\langle\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{Y\xi}({\bf Q})|{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}_{\rm sp}|\mathcal{S}_{X\eta}({\bf P})\rangle.

(96b)

When computing the momentum and spinor parts of the
H
-operator amplitude in Eq.(
96
),
regarding commutability of mode states [cf. Eq.(
8
)],
the most natural assumption is that spinor states satisfy Eq.(
29
) like that in the
case of unit mode, while, momentum states are commutable.

VII.2
H-operator for free process

For brevity, we use
H^{0}_{M}
to indicate
H^{M\to M}_{\rm free}
.
Under momentum conservation, we write
{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}_{\rm mom}
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}^{M\to M}_{\rm mom}=\int d\widetilde{p}d\widetilde{q}|{\bf q}\rangle\lambda_{C}|{\bf p}|\delta^{3}\left({\bf p}-{\bf q}\right)\langle{\bf p}|.

(97)

For
{\mathcal{C}}^{M\to M}_{\rm mom}
to have the dimension of momentum,
the simplest choice of
\lambda_{C}
is that
\lambda_{C}=|{\bf p}|
.
Then, we get that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}^{M\to M}_{\rm mom}=\int d\widetilde{p}|{\bf p}\rangle|{\bf p}|\langle{\bf p}|.

(98)

Meanwhile, the simplest choice for the spinor part is that
{\mathcal{C}}^{M\to M}_{\rm sp}=1
,
which gives that
C^{M\to M}_{\alpha\beta}=\Upsilon_{\beta\alpha}
.
As a result, by direct derivation (Appendix
F.1
), from Eq.(
93
) one gets that

\displaystyle H_{M}^{0}=\int d\widetilde{p}\ |M^{\alpha}_{{\bf p}}\rangle|{\bf p}|\langle\widehat{M}_{{\bf p}\alpha}|.

(99)

Let us compute the value of
p^{0}
for a mode
M
in a state
|M^{\alpha}_{\bf p}\rangle
, as defined in Eq.(
9
).
Due to the
\delta
-function in the normalization condition of
|M^{\alpha}_{\bf p}\rangle
in Eq.(
81
),

p^{0}
should be written as

\displaystyle p^{0}=\int d\widetilde{q}\langle\widehat{M}^{\beta}_{\bf q}|H^{0}_{M}|M^{\alpha}_{\bf p}\rangle\quad\text{with $\beta=\alpha$}.

(100)

Making use of Eq.(
81
), direct computation gives that (Appendix
F.1
)

\displaystyle p^{0}=\Upsilon(\alpha,\alpha)|{\bf p}|.

(101)

For a fermionic mode
F
with
\Upsilon(\alpha,\alpha)
given in Eq.(
VI.4
),
one finds that
p^{0}=\varrho(\alpha)|{\bf p}|
and, hence,

\displaystyle\text{sign of $p^{0}$}=\varrho\quad\text{for fermionic modes}.

(102)

VII.3
H-operators for FIPs

In this section, we discuss
H
-operators of FIPs.
To satisfy momentum conservation, the operator
{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}_{\rm mom}
of an FIP should contain the term of

|{\bf P}_{\Sigma}|\delta^{3}({\bf P}_{\Sigma}-{\bf Q}_{\Sigma})
, where
{\bf P}_{\Sigma}={\bf p}

for one mode and
{\bf P}_{\Sigma}={\bf p}+{\bf p}^{\prime}
for two modes, and similar for
{\bf Q}_{\Sigma}
.
With such a term,
{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}_{\rm mom}
automatically gets the dimension of momentum.
Hence, its simplest form is written as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}_{\rm mom}=\int d\widetilde{\bf P}d\widetilde{\bf Q}|{\bf Q}\rangle|{\bf P}_{\Sigma}|\delta^{3}\left({\bf P}_{\Sigma}-{\bf Q}_{\Sigma}\right)\langle{\bf P}|.

(103)

Let us first discuss FIP-
1
.
Consider an FIP-
1
of
f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B
,
with both
f
and
f^{\prime}
possessing
n_{f}
layers and
B
possessing
n_{B}
layers.
For this process,
Y
is a single mode
B
and, hence,
P_{Y}=I_{B}
.
Then, making use of Eqs.(
95
), (
96b
), and (
103
),
from Eq.(
93
) one gets that

\displaystyle H_{\rm FIP}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}=\int d\Omega\ |B^{\lambda}_{\bf k}\rangle\delta^{3}_{P}C^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\lambda}\langle\widehat{\overline{f}^{\prime}}_{{\bf p}^{\prime}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}|\langle\widehat{f}_{{\bf p}}^{\alpha}|,\

(104)

where
d\Omega=|{\bf k}|d\widetilde{p}d\widetilde{p}^{\prime}d\widetilde{k}
,
\delta^{3}_{P}=\delta^{3}({\bf p}+{\bf p}^{\prime}-{\bf k})
, and

\displaystyle C^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\lambda}=\langle\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{B\lambda}({\bf k})|{\mathcal{C}}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}_{\rm sp}|\mathcal{S}_{f\alpha}({\bf p})\mathcal{S}_{\overline{f}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}({\bf p}^{\prime})\rangle.

(105)

The function of the operator
{\mathcal{C}}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}_{\rm sp}
is to project a spinor

|\mathcal{S}_{f\alpha}({\bf p})\mathcal{S}_{\overline{f}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}({\bf p}^{\prime})\rangle
to
|{\mathcal{S}}_{B\lambda}({\bf k})\rangle
.
To carry out this function, the simplest form is written as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}_{\rm sp}={T}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}\overrightarrow{D}.

(106)

Here,
\overrightarrow{D}
indicates an operation that changes
|\mathcal{S}_{f\alpha}({\bf p})\mathcal{S}_{\overline{f}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}({\bf p}^{\prime})\rangle

to an
n_{f}
-row and one-column matrix; more exact,
with Eqs.(
VI.2
) and (
VI.2
),
\overrightarrow{D}
acts as

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{D}|\mathcal{S}_{f\alpha}({\bf p})\mathcal{S}_{\overline{f}^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}({\bf p}^{\prime})\rangle=\frac{1}{n_{f}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}e^{i(\vartheta^{f}_{1}+\vartheta^{f^{\prime}}_{1})}|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{1}\rangle|{\mathcal{L}}^{\overline{f}^{\prime}}_{1}\rangle\\
e^{i(\vartheta^{f}_{2}+\vartheta^{f^{\prime}}_{2})}|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{2}\rangle|{\mathcal{L}}^{\overline{f}^{\prime}}_{2}\rangle\\
\vdots\\
e^{i(\vartheta^{f}_{n_{f}}+\vartheta^{f^{\prime}}_{n_{f}})}|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{n_{f}}\rangle|{\mathcal{L}}^{\overline{f}^{\prime}}_{n_{f}}\rangle\end{array}\right).

The symbol
{T}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}
projects the result of
\overrightarrow{D}
to spinor states of
B
.
The simplest form of
{T}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}
is written as

\displaystyle{T}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}={P}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}T_{k}.

(111)

Here,
T_{k}
changes an
n_{f}
-row and one-column matrix
to an
n_{B}
-row and one-column matrix, written as an
n_{B}\times n_{f}
matrix,

\displaystyle{T}_{k}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}T_{11}&\cdots&T_{1n_{f}}\\
\vdots&&\\
T_{n_{B}1}&\cdots&T_{n_{B}n_{f}}\end{array}\right).

For different classes of FIPs,
the exact meaning of which will be discussed later in Sec.
VIII
in a specific model of modes,

T_{k}
may take different forms indicated with
k=1,2,\ldots
.

On the rhs of Eq.(
111
),
the function of the symbol
{P}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}
is to change direct products of Weyl spinors,
which lie in rows of the result of
T_{k}\overrightarrow{D}
, to
bosonic spinor states that match the corresponding layers of
B
.
To express this symbol explicitly, we introduce an index
\Theta
for spinors, defined as

\displaystyle\Theta:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&\hbox{for spinors in ${\mathscr{V}}$ or ${\mathscr{W}}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}$;}\\
2,&\hbox{for spinors in ${\mathscr{S}}$ or ${\mathscr{W}}\otimes{\mathscr{W}}$;}\\
3,&\hbox{for spinors in $\overline{{\mathscr{S}}}$ or $\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}$.}\end{array}\right.

To get the above-mentioned matching,

{P}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}
should be proportional to
\delta_{\Theta^{B}_{l}\Theta^{TD}_{l}}
,
where
\Theta^{B}_{l}
indicates the
\Theta
value of the
l
-th layer of the mode
B

and
\Theta^{TD}_{l}
for the
l
-th row of the result of
T_{k}\overrightarrow{D}
.
Furthermore, we note that
s(\overline{s})
-layers of
B
should give no contribution to the physical amplitude
due to Eq.(
47
).
As a result, for brevity one may set

\displaystyle T_{nm}=0,\ \text{if the $n$th layer of $B$ is an $s(\overline{s})$-layer}.

(118)

Then,
{P}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}
includes contributions only from
z(\overline{z})
-layers of
B
.
The only method of linking spinors in
{\mathscr{W}}\otimes\overline{{\mathscr{W}}}
to those in
{\mathscr{V}}

is through the operator
\sigma
in Eq.(
30
).
Thus, with the contribution from
s(\overline{s})
-layers already excluded by Eq.(
118
),

{P}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}
is written as

\displaystyle{P}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}=\sigma\prod_{l=1}^{n_{B}}\delta_{\Theta^{B}_{l}\Theta^{TD}_{l}}.

(119)

Clearly, a nontrivial FIP should satisfy

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{n_{B}}\delta_{\Theta^{B}_{l}\Theta^{TD}_{l}}=1.

(120)

Next, we discuss FIP-
2
.
In the FIP-
2
of
B\to f\overline{f}^{\prime}
,
Y
contains two fermionic modes (
f
and
\overline{f}^{\prime}
).
If one requires that the bra that matches
|Y^{\xi}_{\bf Q}\rangle
in (
91
)
should be an ACC-bra, the simplest choice of
P_{Y}
is the identity operator
I_{Y}
.
However, presently it is unclear whether this is the only natural choice.
The main reason is that, under the momentum-spin-separated form of the operator
{\mathcal{C}}^{X\to Y}
in Eq.(
94
),
the operator
\sigma^{T}
in its spinor part, which is to be used
in a way similar to
\sigma
in Eq.(
119
), is a purely mathematical operator
and does not unnecessarily require an ACC-bra for the spinor part of
P_{Y}
.

We observe that the above-discussed uncertainty in
P_{Y}
may be circumvented,
if one adopts a well known rule in quantum mechanics, which states that the time evolution operator should be Hermitian.
In fact, this rule directly determines
H_{\rm FIP}^{B\to f\overline{f}^{\prime}}
from
H_{\rm FIP}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}
by the relation that

\displaystyle H_{\rm FIP}^{B\to f\overline{f}^{\prime}}=\left(H_{\rm FIP}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}\right)^{+},

(121)

where the superscript “
+
” indicates adjoint operator defined with respect to the ACC-scalar product.
(See Appendix
D.3
for detailed discussions on adjoint operators.)

VII.4
A concise expression of FIP amplitude

In this section, we derive a concise expression for the FIP amplitude

C^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\lambda}
.
Substituting the fermionic state in Eq.(
VII.3
), bosonic state as in Eq.(
VI.3
),
and the operator
{\mathcal{C}}^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}_{\rm sp}
in Eq.(
106
) into Eq.(
105
),
and making use of Eqs.(
43
), (
71
), (
VII.3
), and (
119
),
one gets that

\displaystyle C^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\lambda}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{f}\sqrt{n_{V}}}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{n_{B}}\delta_{\Theta^{B}_{l}\Theta^{TD}_{l}}\right)\sum_{n=1}^{n_{B}}\sum_{m=1}^{n_{f}}\langle\langle\overline{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}({\bf k})|\sigma T_{nm}

\displaystyle\times\exp\Big[i(\vartheta^{f}_{m}+\vartheta^{f^{\prime}}_{m}-\vartheta_{n}^{B})\Big]|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{m}\rangle|{\mathcal{L}}^{\overline{f}^{\prime}}_{m}\rangle.

(122)

On the rhs of Eq.(
122
), due to Eq.(
118
), the label
n
effectively runs only over

z(\overline{z})
-layers of
B
.
Hence, under the nonvanishing condition of FIP in Eq.(
120
),
only two types of the product
|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{m}\rangle|\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}^{f^{\prime}}_{m}\rangle
contribute,
i.e.,
|u^{f}_{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle|\overline{v}^{\overline{f}^{\prime}}_{{\alpha^{\prime}}}({\bf p}^{\prime})\rangle
and

|\overline{v}^{f}_{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle|u^{\overline{f}^{\prime}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}({\bf p}^{\prime})\rangle
.
We use
\mathcal{M}_{z}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\overline{z}}
to denote the sets of the layer number
m

of
f
and
\overline{f}^{\prime}
, corresponding to these two cases, respectively, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{z}:=\{m:|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{m}\rangle|\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}^{f^{\prime}}_{m}\rangle=|u_{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle|\overline{v}_{{\alpha^{\prime}}}({\bf p}^{\prime})\rangle\},

(123a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\overline{z}}:=\{m:|{\mathcal{L}}^{f}_{m}\rangle|\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}^{f^{\prime}}_{m}\rangle=|\overline{v}_{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle|u_{\alpha^{\prime}}({\bf p}^{\prime})\rangle\}.

(123b)

Then, subject to the condition (
120
),
C^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\lambda}
is written as

\displaystyle C^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\lambda}=\frac{1}{n_{f}\sqrt{n_{V}}}\langle\langle\overline{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}({\bf k})|

\displaystyle\sigma\Big(c|u^{f}_{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle|\overline{v}^{\overline{f}^{\prime}}_{{\alpha^{\prime}}}({\bf p}^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle+d|\overline{v}^{f}_{\alpha}({\bf p})\rangle|u^{\overline{f}^{\prime}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}({\bf p}^{\prime})\rangle\Big),

(124)

where
c
and
d
are
c
-numbers determined as follows,

\displaystyle c=\sum_{n=1}^{n_{B}}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{z}}T_{nm}e^{i(\vartheta^{f}_{m}+\vartheta^{f^{\prime}}_{m}-\vartheta_{n}^{B})},

(125a)

\displaystyle d=\sum_{n=1}^{n_{B}}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\overline{z}}}T_{nm}e^{i(\vartheta^{f}_{m}+\vartheta^{f^{\prime}}_{m}-\vartheta_{n}^{B})}.

(125b)

Substituting the operator
\sigma
in Eq.(
30
) into Eq.(
124
),
after some derivation (Appendix
F.2
),
we get the following concise expression of the FIP-
1
amplitude under Eq.(
120
), i.e.,

\displaystyle C^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\lambda}={\overline{\varepsilon}}_{\lambda\mu}({\bf k})V_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}({\bf p}^{\prime})\Gamma^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}\gamma^{\mu}U_{\alpha}({\bf p}),

(126)

where
\overline{\varepsilon}_{\lambda\mu}({\bf k})=\langle\langle\overline{\varepsilon}_{\lambda}({\bf k})|T_{\mu}\rangle={\varepsilon}^{*}_{\lambda\mu}({\bf k})
and
\gamma^{\mu}
is given in Eq.(
C.1
).
On the rhs of Eq.(
126
),

U^{\alpha}({\bf p}),V^{\alpha}({\bf p})
, and
\Gamma^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}
are matrices defined below,

\displaystyle U^{\alpha}({\bf p}):=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u^{\alpha A}({\bf p})\\
\overline{v}_{B^{\prime}}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\end{array}\right),\ V^{\alpha}({\bf p}):=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}v^{\alpha B}({\bf p})\\
-\overline{u}_{A^{\prime}}^{\alpha}({\bf p})\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\Gamma^{f\overline{f}^{\prime}\to B}:=\frac{1}{n_{f}\sqrt{2n_{V}}}\Gamma_{cd},

(131)

where
\Gamma_{cd}
indicates a
2\times 2
matrix to be used often in later discussions, defined by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{cd}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&c\\
d&0\end{array}\right).

VII.5
H-operators for VF and BPIPs

In this section, we discuss
H
-operators for VF and use them to
construct
H
-operators for BPIPs.
